People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Hey, Doc. Yeah, so... I guess I’m here because I just feel like I’m always running hot. Like I literally can’t shut the motor off. Which is funny, because we have this backup rig at the station, Engine 7, and it has this terrible idling issue where it just shakes the whole bay floor. Total piece of junk. Reminds me of this old Ford I drove back when I was getting my associate's degree. Man, that thing burned oil like you wouldn't believe. I had to keep a whole case of it in the trunk just to get to class, and even then it would overheat if I hit traffic on I-95. But, uh... wait, what was I saying? Right, right, my internal motor. It just won't quit, and lately, that’s been getting me in trouble at the firehouse.

He just got engaged to this girl from down in Tampa, so I guess I get it, but still, you gotta pay attention on the job. But yeah, uh... the meetings. I just can't stay planted in that chair. I have to do literally anything besides just sit there.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, all the time. It's actually a huge problem at the station. Like, if we're standing around the rig talking through a plan for a drill or a response, I just... I cut in. The answer is already in my head, and I just spit it out before the other guys even finish their sentence. And then I look around and realize I just stepped on somebody or completely messed up the flow of the discussion. It's like I can't hold it in. Which, speaking of the rig, we just got this new Pierce pumper truck last month. Beautiful piece of equipment. It's got the new Detroit DD13 engine, 500 horsepower. The torque on that thing is insane. We took it out for a pump test down by the reservoir, and the draft capacity is just... I mean, it pulls water so fast. The old truck, we'd be waiting forever to get a good prime, and the primer pump always sounded like it was grinding rocks. I kept telling the mechanic the gears were stripped, but he swore it was fine. Anyway, what was I saying? Oh, right, interrupting. Yeah, I do it a lot. I don't mean to be disrespectful, it just... pops out before my brain catches up.

They said they just can't count on me to track the details consistently. And in our line of work, missing a detail means someone gets hurt, you know? So it's... it's really messing things up for me right now.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned blurting out answers in school — do you still find yourself interrupting people or saying things without thinking them through first, especially when you're excited or frustrated?\n\nPatient: Yeah, all the time. Total piece of junk. Reminds me of this old Ford I drove back when I was getting my associate's degree. Man, that thing burned oil like you wouldn't believe. It just won't quit, and lately, that’s been getting me in trouble at the firehouse.\n\nDoctor: When you say you're 'running hot' and can't shut your motor off, do you feel like your mood is unusually high or great, or is it more that you feel restless and anxious?\n\nPatient: No, it's not like I'm in a super great mood or anything. It's definitely the restless thing. Like, if I have to sit still through one of our command meetings, it just feels like my body is revving way too high. I get this intense need to just stand up, walk the bay, check the rigs... which, honestly, someone needs to be checking the rigs more often anyway, because just last shift, Miller left the driver-side compartment open on the rescue truck. If the tones had dropped and we had to roll out, that door would have been sheared right off on the bay frame. Miller’s a good kid, but man, his head is completely in the clouds lately. He just got engaged to this girl from down in Tampa, so I guess I get it, but still, you gotta pay attention on the job. But yeah, uh... the meetings. I just can't stay planted in that chair. I have to do literally anything besides just sit there.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way — is this something that's been going on for most of your life, or did it start more recently?\n\nPatient: Oh, man. Honestly, most of my life. My mom always says I was exactly like this back in grade school. She's got this whole box of my old report cards in her attic—which, by the way, I told her she needs to get up off the floor because if the roof leaks, that whole box is ruined. We had a call last month, a roof collapse on a two-story colonial because the guy never cleared his gutters and the water just rotted the decking. Whole ceiling came down in the master bedroom. Absolute mess. But anyway, yeah, she kept all these notes from my teachers. They all said the same thing—I was always out of my seat, blurting out answers before the teacher finished the question, losing my homework. It drove her crazy because they'd always say I was bright enough to do the work, I just couldn't keep it together. So yeah, it's been a lifelong thing, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Besides the restlessness and not being able to sit still, do you have trouble concentrating or focusing on tasks — like, do you find your mind wandering a lot, or is it hard to stick with one thing even when you want to?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. It's like my brain just changes the channel on me. Like... okay, so when we have our morning briefings, or lately we've been doing this new drone training for search and rescue. Which, the drones are incredible, man. They have this thermal imaging camera that can spot a heat signature through thick canopy. We used one a couple weeks ago when this hiker got lost up near the ridge. Guy didn't even bring a flashlight, just his cell phone, and of course the battery died. Why do people go hiking at 5 PM with 10% battery? It makes zero sense. But the drone found him in like twenty minutes. Amazing piece of tech. But anyway, what was I... right, focusing. So in the training or the briefings, I'm locked in for the first few minutes, right? I'm listening. But then, suddenly, I realize I've just been staring at the map or the screen for God knows how long, and I didn't catch half of what command just said. It's like I'm looking right at them, but I'm not actually hearing anything.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned these things have been getting you in trouble at the firehouse lately. Can you tell me more about what kind of trouble — like, has this affected your job performance, your relationships with coworkers, or anything like that?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it's... it's getting bad. It's actually starting to cost me trust with the crew, which is the worst part. Like, they pulled me into a performance and safety meeting last week. The chief was there, the captain, the whole deal. Sitting in that office, which, by the way, the AC in the chief's office is completely busted. It was like 85 degrees in there. I told him he needs to get public works down there to check the condenser unit on the roof, because I'm pretty sure the coils are just totally frozen over. That happens all the time with those older commercial units, especially when it's this humid out. You gotta check the filters, too, but nobody ever does. But anyway, yeah, they sat me down and basically said my shot at moving into leadership—which I've been working toward for three years—is slipping. They said they just can't count on me to track the details consistently. And in our line of work, missing a detail means someone gets hurt, you know? So it's... it's really messing things up for me right now.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned blurting out answers in school — do you still find yourself interrupting people or saying things without thinking them through first, especially when you're excited or frustrated?\n\nPatient: Yeah, all the time. It's actually a huge problem at the station. Like, if we're standing around the rig talking through a plan for a drill or a response, I just... I cut in. The answer is already in my head, and I just spit it out before the other guys even finish their sentence. And then I look around and realize I just stepped on somebody or completely messed up the flow of the discussion. It's like I can't hold it in. Which, speaking of the rig, we just got this new Pierce pumper truck last month. Beautiful piece of equipment. It's got the new Detroit DD13 engine, 500 horsepower. The torque on that thing is insane. We took it out for a pump test down by the reservoir, and the draft capacity is just... I mean, it pulls water so fast. The old truck, we'd be waiting forever to get a good prime, and the primer pump always sounded like it was grinding rocks. I kept telling the mechanic the gears were stripped, but he swore it was fine. Anyway, what was I saying? Oh, right, interrupting. Yeah, I do it a lot. I don't mean to be disrespectful, it just... pops out before my brain catches up.\n\nDoctor: When you're trying to organize or plan something—like getting ready for a shift, managing your gear, or keeping track of important tasks—do you find yourself forgetting steps, losing track of what you were doing, or struggling to get things in order, even when you really want to?\n\nPatient: Yeah, organization is... it's a nightmare for me. Getting ready for a shift is just a disaster half the time. Like, I know exactly what I need to do, right? Check the turnout gear, make sure my radio is charged, get my boots set up. But I'll start doing it, and then I'll notice my locker hinge is squeaking. Which, man, those lockers are so old. They're from, like, the 80s. I keep telling the captain we need to put in a requisition for the new vented ones, because the old metal ones just hold all the moisture in. And if you put damp gear in there, it just smells like wet dog for a week. Plus, the new ones have these built-in charging ports for the radios, which is brilliant. You just plug it right into the shelf. I saw them at this fire expo down in the city last year. They had all this crazy new stuff, like these ultra-lightweight composite helmets that feel like you're wearing nothing. I almost bought one on the spot, but they were like six hundred bucks, and my wife would have killed me. But yeah, so I'll be looking at the squeaky hinge, and then I'll go look for some WD-40, and then I'm in the supply closet looking at the inventory, and before I know it, the bells are going off for a call, and my boots are still in the trunk of my car. I just... I can't keep the steps straight.\n\nDoctor: Looking back over the past few years, would you say these attention and restlessness problems have caused real problems in other areas of your life besides work — like in your relationships at home, or anything you've tried to do on your own time?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it definitely spills over into home life. Like, my wife gets so frustrated with me on my days off. We'll try to just have a quiet Sunday, you know? Sit on the couch, watch a movie, or just hang out with the kids. But I just... I can't do it. I'll sit there for maybe ten minutes, and then I'm tapping my foot, or I start talking over the movie, or I just get up because I suddenly remember I need to check the air pressure in the tires. Which, by the way, I just bought this new digital tire inflator for the garage. It's one of those cordless ones that runs on the same 20-volt battery as my drill. It is so much better than dragging the big air compressor hose all the way out to the driveway. That old compressor is so loud, anyway, it sounds like a jackhammer when it kicks on. The neighbors actually complained about it once when I was using it at like 7 AM on a Saturday. I mean, who sleeps past 7 AM anyway? But I guess some people do. The guy next door, Dave, he works in IT or something, he's always up late. He's got this really nice lawn, though. He uses this organic fertilizer that smells terrible for a day, but man, his grass is green. What was I talking about? Oh, right, relaxing at home. Yeah, I just can't do the quiet stuff. I end up out in the garage organizing my tools or just pacing around the house, and my wife's like, \"Can you just sit down for five minutes?\" And I want to, I really do, but my body just won't let me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

  "note": "",
  "is_final": false

No, I don't think so. I mean, I'm always kind of wired, you know? Like I'm always moving, always doing something. But I wouldn't say I ever feel like I don't need sleep or anything like that. Honestly, sleep is a huge issue at the firehouse anyway. You never get a full night. The tones drop at 2 AM, and you're up, adrenaline pumping, trying to get into your gear. And the tones... man, they upgraded the dispatch system last year, and the new tones are so jarring. The old system had this, like, lower pitch that would wake you up but not give you a heart attack. Now it's this high-pitched screech that just rattles your teeth. I was talking to one of the guys at Station 4, and he said they actually put tape over the speaker in their bunk room just to muffle it a little bit. Which is technically against protocol, but I get it. You do what you gotta do. Speaking of Station 4, they just got a brand new rescue truck. It's an absolute beast. It's got the heavy-duty winch on the front, the massive light tower... we did a joint extrication drill with them last month, and the hydraulic tools they have on that thing are incredible. The spreaders just tore through this old Honda Civic like it was tin foil. I wish we had those spreaders on our rig. Ours are like ten years old and they leak fluid if you push them too hard. But yeah, no, I don't get those super high, no-sleep kind of days. I'm just regular restless all the time.

I don't mean to be disrespectful, it just... pops out before my brain catches up.\n\nDoctor: When you're trying to organize or plan something—like getting ready for a shift, managing your gear, or keeping track of important tasks—do you find yourself forgetting steps, losing track of what you were doing, or struggling to get things in order, even when you really want to?\n\nPatient: Yeah, organization is... it's a nightmare for me. Getting ready for a shift is just a disaster half the time. Like, I know exactly what I need to do, right? Check the turnout gear, make sure my radio is charged, get my boots set up. But I'll start doing it, and then I'll notice my locker hinge is squeaking. Which, man, those lockers are so old. They're from, like, the 80s. I keep telling the captain we need to put in a requisition for the new vented ones, because the old metal ones just hold all the moisture in. And if you put damp gear in there, it just smells like wet dog for a week. Plus, the new ones have these built-in charging ports for the radios, which is brilliant. I'll sit there for maybe ten minutes, and then I'm tapping my foot, or I start talking over the movie, or I just get up because I suddenly remember I need to check the air pressure in the tires. Well, I mean, I guess I’ve always been a little scattered, but it hasn’t always been this severe. It really became a massive problem about a year and five months ago. That’s when the department switched us to these longer rotating shifts, and they piled on a ton of extra documentation, plus I got assigned the new drone duties. 

Which, honestly, the drone is incredible. It’s this commercial-grade quadcopter with a dual thermal imaging payload. We took it up during that big commercial warehouse fire last fall, and you could literally see the heat signatures through the roof before it even collapsed. It’s saved us so much time and keeps the guys safer. The only annoying thing is the battery life—you get maybe thirty minutes if it’s windy, and the replacement batteries are like three hundred bucks a pop. The captain keeps arguing with the battalion chief about the budget for them. The chief is this old-school guy who still thinks we should just be doing everything with ladders and hand tools, you know? He doesn't get the tech at all. He still uses a flip phone. I saw him trying to text on it once, just mashing the number keys over and over to get a letter... it was painful to watch. I tried to show him how voice-to-text works on a smartphone, but he just waved me off. 

But, uh, anyway, what were we talking about? Oh, right, if it's always been this bad. Yeah, ever since those longer rotating shifts and the drone stuff and all the extra paperwork started about a year and five months ago, my brain just feels completely fried. I just can't keep up with it all.

Total piece of junk. Reminds me of this old Ford I drove back when I was getting my associate's degree. Man, that thing burned oil like you wouldn't believe. I had to keep a whole case of it in the trunk just to get to class, and even then it would overheat if I hit traffic on I-95. But, uh... wait, what was I saying? Right, right, my internal motor. It's definitely the restless thing. Like, if I have to sit still through one of our command meetings, it just feels like my body is revving way too high. If the tones had dropped and we had to roll out, that door would have been sheared right off on the bay frame. Miller’s a good kid, but man, his head is completely in the clouds lately. Honestly, most of my life. My mom always says I was exactly like this back in grade school. She's got this whole box of my old report cards in her attic—which, by the way, I told her she needs to get up off the floor because if the roof leaks, that whole box is ruined. We had a call last month, a roof collapse on a two-story colonial because the guy never cleared his gutters and the water just rotted the decking. Whole ceiling came down in the master bedroom. Absolute mess. But anyway, yeah, she kept all these notes from my teachers. They all said the same thing—I was always out of my seat, blurting out answers before the teacher finished the question, losing my homework. It drove her crazy because they'd always say I was bright enough to do the work, I just couldn't keep it together. So yeah, it's been a lifelong thing, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Besides the restlessness and not being able to sit still, do you have trouble concentrating or focusing on tasks — like, do you find your mind wandering a lot, or is it hard to stick with one thing even when you want to?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. It's like my brain just changes the channel on me. Like... okay, so when we have our morning briefings, or lately we've been doing this new drone training for search and rescue. Which, the drones are incredible, man. They have this thermal imaging camera that can spot a heat signature through thick canopy. We used one a couple weeks ago when this hiker got lost up near the ridge. Guy didn't even bring a flashlight, just his cell phone, and of course the battery died. Why do people go hiking at 5 PM with 10% battery? It makes zero sense. But the drone found him in like twenty minutes. Amazing piece of tech. But anyway, what was I... right, focusing. So in the training or the briefings, I'm locked in for the first few minutes, right? I'm listening. But then, suddenly, I realize I've just been staring at the map or the screen for God knows how long, and I didn't catch half of what command just said. It's like I'm looking right at them, but I'm not actually hearing anything.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned these things have been getting you in trouble at the firehouse lately. Can you tell me more about what kind of trouble — like, has this affected your job performance, your relationships with coworkers, or anything like that?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it's... it's getting bad. It's actually starting to cost me trust with the crew, which is the worst part. Like, they pulled me into a performance and safety meeting last week. The chief was there, the captain, the whole deal. Sitting in that office, which, by the way, the AC in the chief's office is completely busted. It was like 85 degrees in there. I told him he needs to get public works down there to check the condenser unit on the roof, because I'm pretty sure the coils are just totally frozen over. That happens all the time with those older commercial units, especially when it's this humid out. You gotta check the filters, too, but nobody ever does. But anyway, yeah, they sat me down and basically said my shot at moving into leadership—which I've been working toward for three years—is slipping. They said they just can't count on me to track the details consistently. And in our line of work, missing a detail means someone gets hurt, you know? So it's... it's really messing things up for me right now.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned blurting out answers in school — do you still find yourself interrupting people or saying things without thinking them through first, especially when you're excited or frustrated?\n\nPatient: Yeah, all the time. It's actually a huge problem at the station. Like, if we're standing around the rig talking through a plan for a drill or a response, I just... I cut in. The answer is already in my head, and I just spit it out before the other guys even finish their sentence. And then I look around and realize I just stepped on somebody or completely messed up the flow of the discussion. It's like I can't hold it in. Which, speaking of the rig, we just got this new Pierce pumper truck last month. Beautiful piece of equipment. It's got the new Detroit DD13 engine, 500 horsepower. The torque on that thing is insane. We took it out for a pump test down by the reservoir, and the draft capacity is just... I mean, it pulls water so fast. The old truck, we'd be waiting forever to get a good prime, and the primer pump always sounded like it was grinding rocks. I kept telling the mechanic the gears were stripped, but he swore it was fine. Anyway, what was I saying? Oh, right, interrupting. Yeah, I do it a lot. I don't mean to be disrespectful, it just... pops out before my brain catches up.\n\nDoctor: When you're trying to organize or plan something—like getting ready for a shift, managing your gear, or keeping track of important tasks—do you find yourself forgetting steps, losing track of what you were doing, or struggling to get things in order, even when you really want to?\n\nPatient: Yeah, organization is... it's a nightmare for me. Getting ready for a shift is just a disaster half the time. Like, I know exactly what I need to do, right? Check the turnout gear, make sure my radio is charged, get my boots set up. But I'll start doing it, and then I'll notice my locker hinge is squeaking. Which, man, those lockers are so old. They're from, like, the 80s. I keep telling the captain we need to put in a requisition for the new vented ones, because the old metal ones just hold all the moisture in. And if you put damp gear in there, it just smells like wet dog for a week. Plus, the new ones have these built-in charging ports for the radios, which is brilliant. You just plug it right into the shelf. I saw them at this fire expo down in the city last year. They had all this crazy new stuff, like these ultra-lightweight composite helmets that feel like you're wearing nothing. I almost bought one on the spot, but they were like six hundred bucks, and my wife would have killed me. But yeah, so I'll be looking at the squeaky hinge, and then I'll go look for some WD-40, and then I'm in the supply closet looking at the inventory, and before I know it, the bells are going off for a call, and my boots are still in the trunk of my car. I just... I can't keep the steps straight.\n\nDoctor: Looking back over the past few years, would you say these attention and restlessness problems have caused real problems in other areas of your life besides work — like in your relationships at home, or anything you've tried to do on your own time?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it definitely spills over into home life. Like, my wife gets so frustrated with me on my days off. We'll try to just have a quiet Sunday, you know? Sit on the couch, watch a movie, or just hang out with the kids. But I just... I can't do it. I'll sit there for maybe ten minutes, and then I'm tapping my foot, or I start talking over the movie, or I just get up because I suddenly remember I need to check the air pressure in the tires. Which, by the way, I just bought this new digital tire inflator for the garage. It's one of those cordless ones that runs on the same 20-volt battery as my drill. It is so much better than dragging the big air compressor hose all the way out to the driveway. That old compressor is so loud, anyway, it sounds like a jackhammer when it kicks on. The neighbors actually complained about it once when I was using it at like 7 AM on a Saturday. I mean, who sleeps past 7 AM anyway? But I guess some people do. The guy next door, Dave, he works in IT or something, he's always up late. He's got this really nice lawn, though. He uses this organic fertilizer that smells terrible for a day, but man, his grass is green. What was I talking about? Oh, right, relaxing at home. Yeah, I just can't do the quiet stuff. I end up out in the garage organizing my tools or just pacing around the house, and my wife's like, \"Can you just sit down for five minutes?\" And I want to, I really do, but my body just won't let me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a stretch of time—maybe a few days or longer—where you felt unusually energized, like you needed a lot less sleep than normal, or where your mood felt really high or like you could do anything? Not just restless, but more like everything felt possible and amazing?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I mean, I'm always kind of wired, you know? Like I'm always moving, always doing something. But I wouldn't say I ever feel like I don't need sleep or anything like that. Honestly, sleep is a huge issue at the firehouse anyway. You never get a full night. The tones drop at 2 AM, and you're up, adrenaline pumping, trying to get into your gear. And the tones... man, they upgraded the dispatch system last year, and the new tones are so jarring. The old system had this, like, lower pitch that would wake you up but not give you a heart attack. Now it's this high-pitched screech that just rattles your teeth. I was talking to one of the guys at Station 4, and he said they actually put tape over the speaker in their bunk room just to muffle it a little bit. Which is technically against protocol, but I get it. You do what you gotta do. Speaking of Station 4, they just got a brand new rescue truck. It's an absolute beast. It's got the heavy-duty winch on the front, the massive light tower... we did a joint extrication drill with them last month, and the hydraulic tools they have on that thing are incredible. The spreaders just tore through this old Honda Civic like it was tin foil. I wish we had those spreaders on our rig. Ours are like ten years old and they leak fluid if you push them too hard. But yeah, no, I don't get those super high, no-sleep kind of days. I'm just regular restless all the time.\n\nDoctor: When you think back over your life—say, the last few years or even going back to your twenties—has the impulsivity and the interrupting and the trouble organizing things stayed pretty much the same level the whole time, or have there been periods where it got significantly worse for a while?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean, I guess I’ve always been a little scattered, but it hasn’t always been this severe. It really became a massive problem about a year and five months ago. That’s when the department switched us to these longer rotating shifts, and they piled on a ton of extra documentation, plus I got assigned the new drone duties. \n\nWhich, honestly, the drone is incredible. It’s this commercial-grade quadcopter with a dual thermal imaging payload. We took it up during that big commercial warehouse fire last fall, and you could literally see the heat signatures through the roof before it even collapsed. It’s saved us so much time and keeps the guys safer. The only annoying thing is the battery life—you get maybe thirty minutes if it’s windy, and the replacement batteries are like three hundred bucks a pop. The captain keeps arguing with the battalion chief about the budget for them. The chief is this old-school guy who still thinks we should just be doing everything with ladders and hand tools, you know? He doesn't get the tech at all. He still uses a flip phone. I saw him trying to text on it once, just mashing the number keys over and over to get a letter... it was painful to watch. I tried to show him how voice-to-text works on a smartphone, but he just waved me off. \n\nBut, uh, anyway, what were we talking about? Oh, right, if it's always been this bad. Yeah, ever since those longer rotating shifts and the drone stuff and all the extra paperwork started about a year and five months ago, my brain just feels completely fried. I just can't keep up with it all.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

  "candidates": ["F90.9"],
  "note": "",
  "is_final": false
}

Honestly, it’s always been a problem, way before my twenties even. Like, my mom says I was exactly like this back in grade school. She always reminds me how I was constantly out of my seat, blurting out answers, losing my homework... she was always getting notes sent home from my teachers, even though they all said I was bright enough to do the work. I just couldn't keep my act together. 

Which, actually, my mom was just going through all those old report cards and notes because she’s getting ready to sell the house. It's the house I grew up in, built back in the seventies, and the plumbing is just a complete nightmare at this point. She had a guy come out to fix a leak under the kitchen sink last week, and he had to tear out the entire bottom cabinet because the wood was totally rotted through. And you know how crazy expensive lumber is right now? It's unbelievable. I went to Home Depot last weekend just to get some basic two-by-fours to build some new shelves in my garage, and I took one look at the prices and just walked right back out. 

I ended up buying those heavy-duty plastic storage bins instead. You know the ones? The black ones with the yellow lids? They are fantastic. They stack so perfectly. We actually use the exact same ones at the firehouse to store all our extra winter gear because they're basically waterproof. The probie accidentally backed over one with the chief's buggy last month and the bin didn't even crack. The chief was absolutely furious about the scratch on the buggy, though, he made the kid wash every vehicle in the bay for a week. 

But, uh... wait, what did you ask me? Something about my twenties? Yeah, no, it's always been pretty bad, ever since I was a kid.

That happens all the time with those older commercial units, especially when it's this humid out. You gotta check the filters, too, but nobody ever does. I just couldn't keep my act together. \n\nWhich, actually, my mom was just going through all those old report cards and notes because she’s getting ready to sell the house.
